					<div id="contentSub"><div id="mw-content-subtitle"><span class="mw-redirectedfrom">(Redirected from <a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_Loyola&amp;redirect=no" class="mw-redirect" title="Ignatius Loyola">Ignatius Loyola</a>)</span></div></div>
					
					
					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">Spanish Catholic priest and theologian (1491–1556)</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">In this <a href="/wiki/Spanish_name" class="mw-redirect" title="Spanish name">Spanish name</a>, the first or paternal&#32;<a href="/wiki/Surname" title="Surname">surname</a> is <i> de Oñaz</i>&#32;and the second or maternal family name is <i> Loyola</i>.</div>
<p class="mw-empty-elt">
</p>

<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Francis_of_Assisi" title="Francis of Assisi">Francis of Assisi</a></li>

</div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><span class="nowrap">Major works</span></th><td class="infobox-data"><i><a href="/wiki/Spiritual_Exercises" title="Spiritual Exercises">Spiritual Exercises</a></i></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-full-data"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"></td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header">Signature</th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-full-data"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Signatur_Ignatius_von_Loyola.PNG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/f/fb/Signatur_Ignatius_von_Loyola.PNG" decoding="async" width="150" height="89" class="mw-file-element" data-file-width="122" data-file-height="72" /></a></span></td></tr><tr style="display:none"><td colspan="2">
</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Ignatius of Loyola</b> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:85%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Society_of_Jesus" class="mw-redirect" title="Society of Jesus">SJ</a></span> (<a href="/wiki/Basque_language" title="Basque language">Basque</a>: <i lang="eu">Ignazio Loiolakoa</i>; <a href="/wiki/Spanish_language" title="Spanish language">Spanish</a>: <i lang="es">Ignacio de Loyola</i>; <a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Ignatius de Loyola</i>; born <b>Íñigo López de Oñaz y Loyola</b>; <span title="circa">c.</span><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;23 October 1491</span><sup id="cite_ref-RGV_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-RGV-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> – 31 July 1556), venerated as <b>Saint Ignatius of Loyola</b>, was a Spanish Catholic priest and theologian, who, with six companions, founded the religious order of the <a href="/wiki/Society_of_Jesus" class="mw-redirect" title="Society of Jesus">Society of Jesus</a> (The Jesuits), and became its first <a href="/wiki/Superior_General_of_the_Society_of_Jesus" title="Superior General of the Society of Jesus">Superior General</a>, in Paris in 1541.<sup id="cite_ref-Idig_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Idig-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Ignatius envisioned the purpose of the Society of Jesus to be <a href="/wiki/Missionary" title="Missionary">missionary work</a> and <a href="/wiki/Education" title="Education">teaching</a>. In addition to the vows of chastity, obedience and poverty of other religious orders in the church, Loyola instituted a fourth vow for Jesuits of obedience to the Pope, to engage in projects ordained by the pontiff.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Jesuits were instrumental in leading the <a href="/wiki/Counter-Reformation" title="Counter-Reformation">Counter-Reformation</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As a former soldier, Ignatius paid particular attention to the spiritual formation of his recruits and recorded his method in the <i><a href="/wiki/Spiritual_Exercises" title="Spiritual Exercises">Spiritual Exercises</a></i> (1548). In time, the method has become known as <a href="/wiki/Ignatian_spirituality" title="Ignatian spirituality">Ignatian spirituality</a>. He was <a href="/wiki/Beatified" class="mw-redirect" title="Beatified">beatified</a> in 1609 and was <a href="/wiki/Canonized" class="mw-redirect" title="Canonized">canonized</a> as a saint on 12 March 1622. His feast day is celebrated on 31 July. He is the <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saint</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Basque_Country_(greater_region)" title="Basque Country (greater region)">Basque</a> provinces of <a href="/wiki/Gipuzkoa" title="Gipuzkoa">Gipuzkoa</a> and <a href="/wiki/Biscay" title="Biscay">Biscay</a> as well as of the Society of Jesus. He was declared patron saint of all spiritual retreats by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XI" title="Pope Pius XI">Pope Pius XI</a> in 1922.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Loyola&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p><b>Ignatius of Loyola</b> was born <b>Iñigo López de Oñaz y Loyola</b> in the castle at Loyola, in the municipality of <a href="/wiki/Azpeitia" title="Azpeitia">Azpeitia</a>, Gipuzkoa, in the <a href="/wiki/Basque_Country_(greater_region)" title="Basque Country (greater region)">Basque region of Spain</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-cathenc_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cathenc-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> His parents, Don Beltrán Ibáñez de Oñaz y Loyola and Doña María (or Marina) Sáenz de Licona y Balda, who were of the minor nobility,<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> from the clan of Loyola, were involved in the <a href="/wiki/War_of_the_Bands" title="War of the Bands">Basque war of the bands</a>. Their <a href="/wiki/Manor_house" title="Manor house">manor house</a> was demolished on the orders of the King of Castile in 1456 for their depredations in Gipuzkoa, with Iñigo's paternal grandfather being expelled to Andalusia by <a href="/wiki/Henry_IV_of_Castile" title="Henry IV of Castile">Henry IV</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> Íñigo was the youngest of their thirteen children. Their eldest son, Juan Pérez, had soldiered in forces commanded by <a href="/wiki/Gonzalo_Fern%C3%A1ndez_de_C%C3%B3rdoba" title="Gonzalo Fernández de Córdoba">Gonzalo Fernández de Córdoba</a>, but died fighting in the <a href="/wiki/Italian_Wars" title="Italian Wars">Italian Wars</a> (1494–1559).<sup id="cite_ref-Brodrick_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brodrick-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Santuario_De_Loyola,_Basque_Country,_Spain.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Santuario_De_Loyola%2C_Basque_Country%2C_Spain.jpg/250px-Santuario_De_Loyola%2C_Basque_Country%2C_Spain.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="333" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Santuario_De_Loyola%2C_Basque_Country%2C_Spain.jpg/375px-Santuario_De_Loyola%2C_Basque_Country%2C_Spain.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Santuario_De_Loyola%2C_Basque_Country%2C_Spain.jpg/500px-Santuario_De_Loyola%2C_Basque_Country%2C_Spain.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1536" data-file-height="2048" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Sanctuary_of_Loyola" title="Sanctuary of Loyola">Sanctuary of Loyola</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Azpeitia" title="Azpeitia">Azpeitia</a>, built atop the birthplace of the saint.</figcaption></figure>
<p>He was baptized "Íñigo" on honour of <a href="/wiki/%C3%8D%C3%B1igo_of_O%C3%B1a" title="Íñigo of Oña">Íñigo of Oña</a>, Abbot of <a href="/wiki/O%C3%B1a" title="Oña">Oña</a>; the name also is a medieval Basque diminutive for "My little one".<sup id="cite_ref-cathenc_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cathenc-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> It is not clear when he began using the Latin name "Ignatius" instead of his baptismal name "Íñigo".<sup id="cite_ref-Verd1_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Verd1-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> Historian Gabriel María Verd says that Íñigo did not intend to change his name, but rather adopted a name which he believed was a simple variant of his own, for use in France and Italy where it was better understood.<sup id="cite_ref-Verd2_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Verd2-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Íñigo adopted the surname "de Loyola" in reference to the Basque village of Loyola where he was born.<sup id="cite_ref-encyc_brit_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-encyc_brit-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Soon after the birth of Íñigo, his mother died. Maternal care fell to María de Garín, the wife of the local blacksmith.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> In 1498, his second eldest brother, Martin, heir to the estate, took his new wife to live in the castle, and she became mistress of the household. Later, the seven-year-old boy Íñigo returned to Casa Loyola. Anticipating his possible ecclesiastic career, Don Beltrán had Íñigo <a href="/wiki/Tonsure" title="Tonsure">tonsured</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Brodrick_10-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brodrick-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Military_career">Military career</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Loyola&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Military career">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ignatius_of_Loyola_(militant).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/Ignatius_of_Loyola_%28militant%29.jpg/220px-Ignatius_of_Loyola_%28militant%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="297" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/Ignatius_of_Loyola_%28militant%29.jpg/330px-Ignatius_of_Loyola_%28militant%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/Ignatius_of_Loyola_%28militant%29.jpg/440px-Ignatius_of_Loyola_%28militant%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1143" data-file-height="1543" /></a><figcaption>Ignatius in his armour, in a 16th-century painting</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint_Ignatius_of_Loyola%27s_Vision_of_Christ_and_God_the_Father_at_La_Storta_LACMA_M.89.59.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a3/Saint_Ignatius_of_Loyola%27s_Vision_of_Christ_and_God_the_Father_at_La_Storta_LACMA_M.89.59.jpg/220px-Saint_Ignatius_of_Loyola%27s_Vision_of_Christ_and_God_the_Father_at_La_Storta_LACMA_M.89.59.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="376" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a3/Saint_Ignatius_of_Loyola%27s_Vision_of_Christ_and_God_the_Father_at_La_Storta_LACMA_M.89.59.jpg/330px-Saint_Ignatius_of_Loyola%27s_Vision_of_Christ_and_God_the_Father_at_La_Storta_LACMA_M.89.59.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a3/Saint_Ignatius_of_Loyola%27s_Vision_of_Christ_and_God_the_Father_at_La_Storta_LACMA_M.89.59.jpg/440px-Saint_Ignatius_of_Loyola%27s_Vision_of_Christ_and_God_the_Father_at_La_Storta_LACMA_M.89.59.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1962" data-file-height="3351" /></a><figcaption><i>Saint Ignatius of Loyola's Vision of Christ and God the Father at La Storta</i> by <a href="/wiki/Domenichino" title="Domenichino">Domenichino</a><sup id="cite_ref-domenichino_painting_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-domenichino_painting-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>Instead, Íñigo became a <a href="/wiki/Page_(occupation)" class="mw-redirect" title="Page (occupation)">page</a> in the service of a relative, Juan Velázquez de Cuéllar, treasurer (<i>contador mayor</i>) of the kingdom of <a href="/wiki/Crown_of_Castile" title="Crown of Castile">Castile</a>. During his time in the household of Don Velázquez, Íñigo took up dancing, fencing, gambling, the pursuit of the young ladies, and duelling.<sup id="cite_ref-Brodrick_10-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brodrick-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Íñigo was keen on military exercises and was driven by a desire for fame. He patterned his life after the stories of <a href="/wiki/El_Cid" title="El Cid">El Cid</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Round_Table" title="Round Table">knights of Camelot</a>, <i><a href="/wiki/The_Song_of_Roland" class="mw-redirect" title="The Song of Roland">The Song of Roland</a></i> and other tales of romantic chivalry.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He joined the army at seventeen, and according to one biographer, he strutted about "with his cape flying open to reveal his tight-fitting hose and boots; a sword and dagger at his waist".<sup id="cite_ref-sword_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-sword-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (July 2020)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> According to another he was "a fancy dresser, an expert dancer, a womanizer, sensitive to insult, and a rough punkish swordsman who used his privileged status to escape prosecution for violent crimes committed with his priest brother at carnival time."<sup id="cite_ref-Traub_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Traub-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1509, aged 18, Íñigo took up arms for <a href="/wiki/Antonio_Manrique_de_Lara,_2nd_Duke_of_N%C3%A1jera" title="Antonio Manrique de Lara, 2nd Duke of Nájera">Antonio Manrique de Lara, 2nd Duke of Nájera</a>. His diplomacy and leadership qualities earned him the title "servant of the court", and made him very useful to the Duke.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
Under the Duke's leadership, Íñigo participated in many battles without injury. However at the <a href="/wiki/List_of_battles_of_the_Italian_Wars#Pampeluna" title="List of battles of the Italian Wars">Battle of Pamplona</a> on 20 May 1521 he was gravely injured when <a href="/wiki/Spanish_conquest_of_Iberian_Navarre#1521_French-Navarrese_expedition" title="Spanish conquest of Iberian Navarre">a French-Navarrese expedition force</a> stormed the fortress of Pamplona, and a <a href="/wiki/Round_shot" title="Round shot">cannonball</a> ricocheting off a nearby wall canonizing his right leg.<sup id="cite_ref-j1_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-j1-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
Íñigo was returned to his father's castle in Loyola, where, in an era before <a href="/wiki/Anesthetic" title="Anesthetic">anesthetics</a>, he underwent several surgical operations to repair the leg, with his bones set and rebroken. In the end, the operations left his right leg shorter than the other. He would limp for the rest of his life, with his military career over.<sup id="cite_ref-Traub_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Traub-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religious_conversion_and_visions">Religious conversion and visions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Loyola&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Religious conversion and visions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>

<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1045330069"><table class="sidebar nomobile nowraplinks"><tbody><tr><td class="sidebar-pretitle">Part of <a href="/wiki/Category:Society_of_Jesus" title="Category:Society of Jesus">a series</a> on the</td></tr><tr><th class="sidebar-title-with-pretitle"><a href="/wiki/Jesuits" title="Jesuits">Society of Jesus</a></th></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-image"><span typeof="mw:File/Frameless"><a href="/wiki/File:Ihs-logo.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/Ihs-logo.svg/130px-Ihs-logo.svg.png" decoding="async" width="130" height="130" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/Ihs-logo.svg/195px-Ihs-logo.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/Ihs-logo.svg/260px-Ihs-logo.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="567" data-file-height="567" /></a></span><div class="sidebar-caption"><a href="/wiki/Christogram" title="Christogram">Christogram</a> of the Jesuits</div></td></tr><tr><th class="sidebar-heading" style="background-color:gold">

</tr><tr><th class="sidebar-heading" style="background-color:gold">
<a href="/wiki/List_of_Jesuits" title="List of Jesuits">Notable Jesuits</a></th></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-content hlist">

<li><a href="/wiki/List_of_saints_of_the_Society_of_Jesus" title="List of saints of the Society of Jesus">Jesuit saints</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/List_of_Jesuit_theologians" title="List of Jesuit theologians">Jesuit theologians</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Category:Jesuit_philosophers" title="Category:Jesuit philosophers">Jesuit philosophers</a></li></ul></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-below">
<span class="mw-image-border noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:046CupolaSPietro.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="icon" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/046CupolaSPietro.jpg/16px-046CupolaSPietro.jpg" decoding="async" width="16" height="12" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/046CupolaSPietro.jpg/24px-046CupolaSPietro.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/046CupolaSPietro.jpg/32px-046CupolaSPietro.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="600" /></a></span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Catholicism" class="mw-redirect" title="Portal:Catholicism">Catholicism&#32;portal</a></td></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1063604349"><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Society_of_Jesus" title="Template:Society of Jesus"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Society_of_Jesus" title="Template talk:Society of Jesus"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Society_of_Jesus&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>While recovering from surgery, Íñigo underwent a spiritual conversion and discerned a call to the religious life. In order to divert the weary hours of convalescence, he asked for the romances of chivalry, his favourite reading, but there were none in the castle, and instead his beloved sister-in-law, Magdalena de Araoz brought him the lives of Christ and of the saints.<sup id="cite_ref-cathenc_7-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cathenc-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The religious work which most particularly struck him was the <i><a href="/wiki/Vita_Christi" title="Vita Christi">De Vita Christi</a></i> of <a href="/wiki/Ludolph_of_Saxony" title="Ludolph of Saxony">Ludolph of Saxony</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> This book would influence his whole life, inspiring him to devote himself to God and follow the example of <a href="/wiki/Francis_of_Assisi" title="Francis of Assisi">Francis of Assisi</a> and other great monks. It also inspired his method of meditation, since Ludolph proposes that the reader place himself mentally at the scene of the Gospel story, visualising the crib at the Nativity, etc. This type of meditation, known as Simple Contemplation, was the basis for the method that Ignatius outlined in his <i><a href="/wiki/Spiritual_Exercises" title="Spiritual Exercises">Spiritual Exercises</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Aside from dreaming about imitating the saints in his readings, Íñigo was still wandering off in his mind about what "he would do in service to his king and in honour of the royal lady he was in love with". Cautiously he came to realize the after-effect of both kinds of his dreams. He experienced desolation and dissatisfaction when the romantic heroism dream was over, but, the saintly dream ended with much joy and peace. It was the first time he learned about <a href="/wiki/Discernment_in_Christianity" class="mw-redirect" title="Discernment in Christianity">discernment</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Traub_19-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Traub-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After he had recovered sufficiently to walk again, Íñigo resolved to begin a pilgrimage to the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Land" title="Holy Land">Holy Land</a> to "kiss the earth where our Lord had walked",<sup id="cite_ref-Traub_19-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Traub-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> and to do stricter <a href="/wiki/Penance" title="Penance">penances</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Heydt_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Heydt-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> He thought that his plan was confirmed by a vision of the Virgin Mary and the infant Jesus he experienced one night, which resulted in much consolation to him.<sup id="cite_ref-Heydt_27-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Heydt-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> In March 1522, he visited the Benedictine monastery of <a href="/wiki/Santa_Maria_de_Montserrat" class="mw-redirect" title="Santa Maria de Montserrat">Santa Maria de Montserrat</a>. There, he carefully examined his past <a href="/wiki/Sins" class="mw-redirect" title="Sins">sins</a>, <a href="/wiki/Confession_(Catholic_Church)" class="mw-redirect" title="Confession (Catholic Church)">confessed</a>, gave his fine clothes to the poor he met, wore a "garment of sack-cloth", then hung his sword and dagger at <a href="/wiki/Virgin_of_Montserrat" title="Virgin of Montserrat">the Virgin</a>'s altar during an overnight vigil at the shrine.<sup id="cite_ref-cathenc_7-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cathenc-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>From Montserrat he walked on to the nearby town of <a href="/wiki/Manresa" title="Manresa">Manresa</a> (<a href="/wiki/Catalonia" title="Catalonia">Catalonia</a>), where he lived for about a year, begging for his keep, and then eventually doing chores at a local hospital in exchange for food and lodging. For several months he spent much of his time praying in a <a href="/wiki/Cave_of_Saint_Ignatius" title="Cave of Saint Ignatius">cave nearby</a> where he practised rigorous <a href="/wiki/Asceticism" title="Asceticism">asceticism</a>, praying for seven hours a day, and formulating the fundamentals of his <i>Spiritual Exercises</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-cave_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cave-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Íñigo also experienced a series of visions in full daylight while at the hospital. These repeated visions appeared as "a form in the air near him and this form gave him much consolation because it was exceedingly beautiful ... it somehow seemed to have the shape of a serpent and had many things that shone like eyes, but were not eyes. He received much delight and consolation from gazing upon this object ... but when the object vanished he became disconsolate".<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>  He came to interpret this vision as diabolical in nature.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Period_of_studies">Period of studies</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Loyola&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Period of studies">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>In September 1523, Íñigo made a pilgrimage to the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Land" title="Holy Land">Holy Land</a> with the aim of settling there. He remained there from 3 to 23 September but was sent back to Europe by the <a href="/wiki/Franciscans" title="Franciscans">Franciscans</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He returned to Barcelona and at the age of 33 attended a free public grammar school in preparation for university entrance. He went on to the University of Alcalá,<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> where he studied <a href="/wiki/Theology" title="Theology">theology</a> and <a href="/wiki/Latin-language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin-language">Latin</a> from 1526 to 1527.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>There he encountered a number of devout women who had been called before the <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Inquisition" title="Spanish Inquisition">Inquisition</a>. These women were considered <i><a href="/wiki/Alumbrados" title="Alumbrados">alumbrados</a></i>  – a group linked in their zeal and spirituality to Franciscan reforms, but they had incurred mounting suspicion from the administrators of the Inquisition. Once when Íñigo was preaching on the street, three of these devout women began to experience ecstatic states. "One fell senseless, another sometimes rolled about on the ground, another had been seen in the grip of convulsions or shuddering and sweating in anguish." The suspicious activity took place while Íñigo had preached without a degree in theology. As a result, he was singled out for interrogation by the Inquisition, but was later released.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following these risky activities, Íñigo (by this time, he had changed his name to Ignatius, probably to make it more acceptable to other Europeans) <sup id="cite_ref-Verd2_13-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Verd2-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> adopted the surname "de Loyola" in reference to the Basque village of Loyola where he was born.<sup id="cite_ref-encyc_brit_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-encyc_brit-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> moved to France to study at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Paris" title="University of Paris">University of Paris</a>. He attended first the ascetic <a href="/wiki/Coll%C3%A8ge_de_Montaigu" title="Collège de Montaigu">Collège de Montaigu</a>, moving on to the <a href="/wiki/Coll%C3%A8ge_Sainte-Barbe" title="Collège Sainte-Barbe">Collège Sainte-Barbe</a> to study for a master's degree.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He arrived in France at a time of anti-Protestant turmoil which had forced <a href="/wiki/John_Calvin" title="John Calvin">John Calvin</a> to flee France. Very soon after, Ignatius had gathered around him six companions, all of them fellow students at the university.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> They were the Spaniards <a href="/wiki/Alfonso_Salmeron" title="Alfonso Salmeron">Alfonso Salmeron</a>, <a href="/wiki/Diego_Laynez" title="Diego Laynez">Diego Laynez</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Nicholas_Bobadilla" title="Nicholas Bobadilla">Nicholas Bobadilla</a>, with the Portuguese <a href="/wiki/Sim%C3%A3o_Rodrigues" title="Simão Rodrigues">Simão Rodrigues</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Basques" title="Basques">Basque</a>, <a href="/wiki/Francis_Xavier" title="Francis Xavier">Francis Xavier</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Peter_Faber" title="Peter Faber">Peter Faber</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Sardinia_(1720%E2%80%931861)" title="Kingdom of Sardinia (1720–1861)">Savoyard</a>, the latter two becoming his first companions,<sup id="cite_ref-Traub_19-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Traub-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> and his closest associates in the foundation of the future Jesuit order.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>"On the morning of the 15th of August, 1534, in the chapel of <a href="/wiki/Saint-Pierre_de_Montmartre" title="Saint-Pierre de Montmartre">church of Saint Peter</a>, at Montmartre, Loyola and his six companions, of whom only one was a priest, met and took upon themselves the solemn vows of their lifelong work."<sup id="cite_ref-John_Clarke_Ridpath_pp.238_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-John_Clarke_Ridpath_pp.238-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ignatius gained a <a href="/wiki/Magister_degree" title="Magister degree">Magisterium</a> from the University of Paris at the age of forty-three in 1535. In later life he would often be called "Master Ignatius" because of this.<sup id="cite_ref-John_Clarke_Ridpath_pp.238_39-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-John_Clarke_Ridpath_pp.238-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Foundation_of_the_Jesuit_order">Foundation of the Jesuit order</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Loyola&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Foundation of the Jesuit order">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>In 1539, with Peter Faber and Francis Xavier, Ignatius formed the Society of Jesus, which was approved in 1540 by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_III" title="Pope Paul III">Pope Paul III</a>. He was chosen as the first <a href="/wiki/Superior_General_of_the_Society_of_Jesus" title="Superior General of the Society of Jesus">Superior General</a> of the order and invested with the title of "Father General" by the Jesuits.<sup id="cite_ref-encyc_brit_14-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-encyc_brit-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ignatius sent his companions on missions across Europe to create schools, colleges, and seminaries. <a href="/wiki/Juan_de_Vega" class="mw-redirect" title="Juan de Vega">Juan de Vega</a>, then ambassador of <a href="/wiki/Charles_V,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Charles V, Holy Roman Emperor">Charles V, Holy Roman Emperor</a> in Rome, met Ignatius there and having formed a good impression of the Jesuits, invited them to travel with him to his new appointment as <a href="/wiki/Viceroy_of_Sicily" class="mw-redirect" title="Viceroy of Sicily">Viceroy of Sicily</a>. As a result, a Jesuit college was opened in <a href="/wiki/Messina" title="Messina">Messina</a>, which proved a success, so that its rules and methods were later copied in subsequent colleges.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> In a letter to Francis Xavier before his departure to India in 1541, Ignatius famously used the Latin phrase "Ite, inflammate omnia", meaning, "Go, set the world on fire", a phrase used in the Jesuit order to this day.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>With the assistance of his personal secretary, <a href="/wiki/Juan_Alfonso_de_Polanco" title="Juan Alfonso de Polanco">Juan Alfonso de Polanco</a>, Ignatius wrote the Jesuit Constitutions, which were adopted in 1553. They created a centralised organisation of the order,<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> and stressed absolute self-denial and obedience to the Pope and to superiors in the Church hierarchy. This was summarised in the motto <i>perinde ac cadaver</i> – "as if a dead body",<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> meaning that a Jesuit should be as emptied of ego as is a corpse.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> However the overarching Jesuit principle became: <i><a href="/wiki/Ad_maiorem_Dei_gloriam" title="Ad maiorem Dei gloriam">Ad maiorem Dei gloriam</a></i> ("for the greater glory of God").<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2019)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<ul class="gallery mw-gallery-traditional">
		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 205px">
			<div class="thumb" style="width: 200px; height: 230px;"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Ignatius_Loyola_by_Francisco_Zurbaran.jpg" class="mw-file-description" title="Ignatius as Superior General"><img alt="Ignatius as Superior General" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Ignatius_Loyola_by_Francisco_Zurbaran.jpg/170px-Ignatius_Loyola_by_Francisco_Zurbaran.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="194" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Ignatius_Loyola_by_Francisco_Zurbaran.jpg/255px-Ignatius_Loyola_by_Francisco_Zurbaran.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Ignatius_Loyola_by_Francisco_Zurbaran.jpg/340px-Ignatius_Loyola_by_Francisco_Zurbaran.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1696" data-file-height="1931" /></a></span></div>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Death_and_canonization">Death and canonization</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Loyola&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Death and canonization">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Ignatius died in Rome on 31 July 1556, probably of the "<a href="/wiki/Roman_Fever_(disease)" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Fever (disease)">Roman Fever</a>", a severe variant of <a href="/wiki/Malaria" title="Malaria">malaria</a> which was endemic in Rome throughout medieval history. An autopsy revealed that he also had kidney and bladder stones, a probable cause of the abdominal pains he suffered from in later life.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (July 2020)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The anatomist <a href="/wiki/Realdo_Colombo" title="Realdo Colombo">Matteo Colombo</a> was present at the necropsy of St. Ignatius. He describes the results in his <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">De re anatomica libre XV</i></span>:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>I have taken out innumerable stones with my own hands, with various colors found in the kidneys, in the lungs, in the liver, and in the portal vein. For I saw stones in the ureters, in the bladder, in the colon, in the hemorrhoidal veins as well as in the umbilicus. Also in the gall bladder I found stones of various shapes and colors.</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8202;<cite>Matthew Colombo, <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">De re anatomica libre XV</i></span><sup id="cite_ref-Ficarra1942_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ficarra1942-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup></cite></div></blockquote>
<p>From the facts presented, the exact cause of death cannot be established. The stones mentioned in the kidneys, ureters, urinary bladder and gall bladder appear to indicate <a href="/wiki/Nephrolithiasis" class="mw-redirect" title="Nephrolithiasis">nephrolithiasis</a> and <a href="/wiki/Cholelithiasis" class="mw-redirect" title="Cholelithiasis">cholelithiasis</a>. The so-called stones in the veins appear to be thrombosed hemorrhoids. Those mentioned in the colon, liver, and lungs suggest the possibility of a malignant gastro-intestinal growth with metastases to the liver and lungs. Because of the inadequacy of the protocols of the sixteenth century, the exact final anatomical diagnosis on the autopsy of Ignatius cannot be established beyond doubt.<sup id="cite_ref-Ficarra1942_47-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ficarra1942-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His body was dressed in his priestly robes and placed in a wooden coffin and buried in the crypt of the Maria della Strada Church on 1 August 1556. In 1568 the church was demolished and replaced with the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_the_Ges%C3%B9" title="Church of the Gesù">Church of the Gesù</a>. Ignatius' remains were reinterred in the new church in a new coffin.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin2013_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin2013-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ignatius was beatified by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_V" title="Pope Paul V">Pope Paul V</a> on 27 July 1609, and <a href="/wiki/Canonization" title="Canonization">canonized</a> by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gregory_XV" title="Pope Gregory XV">Pope Gregory XV</a> on 12 March 1622.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> His feast day is celebrated annually on 31 July, the day he died. He is venerated as the patron saint of Catholic soldiers, the <a href="/wiki/Military_Ordinariate_of_the_Philippines" title="Military Ordinariate of the Philippines">Military Ordinariate of the Philippines</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Baltimore" title="Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Baltimore">Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Baltimore</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> in his native Basque Country, the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Antwerp" title="Roman Catholic Diocese of Antwerp">Roman Catholic Diocese of Antwerp</a>, Belo Horizonte, Junín, and Rome.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Loyola&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Numerous institutions across the world are named for him, including many <a href="/wiki/Loyola_(disambiguation)#Education" class="mw-redirect mw-disambig" title="Loyola (disambiguation)">educational institutions</a> and Ateneo Universities institutions in the Philippines.
</p><p>In 1852, <a href="/wiki/Loyola_University_Maryland" title="Loyola University Maryland">Loyola University Maryland</a> was the first university in the United States to bear his name.
</p><p>In 1949 he was the subject of a Spanish biographical film <i><a href="/wiki/Loyola,_the_Soldier_Saint" title="Loyola, the Soldier Saint">Loyola, the Soldier Saint</a></i> starring <a href="/wiki/Rafael_Dur%C3%A1n_(actor)" title="Rafael Durán (actor)">Rafael Durán</a> in the role of Ignatius.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2018)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>In 2016, he was the subject of a Filipino film, <i><a href="/wiki/Ignacio_de_Loyola" title="Ignacio de Loyola">Ignacio de Loyola</a></i>, in which he was portrayed by <a href="/wiki/Andreas_Mu%C3%B1oz" title="Andreas Muñoz">Andreas Muñoz</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ignatius of Loyola is honoured in the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">Church of England</a> and in the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Episcopal_Church)" title="Calendar of saints (Episcopal Church)">Episcopal Church</a> on 31 July.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Temple_San_Ignacio_de_Loyola" title="Temple San Ignacio de Loyola">Saint Ignatius de Loyola Catholic Church</a>, built in 1905 in <a href="/wiki/El_Paso,_Texas" title="El Paso, Texas">El Paso, Texas</a>, is named for him.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Genealogy">Genealogy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Loyola&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Genealogy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Blason_d%27Ignace_de_Loyola.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Blason_d%27Ignace_de_Loyola.svg/150px-Blason_d%27Ignace_de_Loyola.svg.png" decoding="async" width="150" height="190" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Blason_d%27Ignace_de_Loyola.svg/225px-Blason_d%27Ignace_de_Loyola.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Blason_d%27Ignace_de_Loyola.svg/300px-Blason_d%27Ignace_de_Loyola.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="603" data-file-height="763" /></a><figcaption>Original shield of Oñaz-Loyola.</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span id="Shield_of_O.C3.B1az-Loyola"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Shield_of_Oñaz-Loyola">Shield of Oñaz-Loyola</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Loyola&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Shield of Oñaz-Loyola">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The Shield of Oñaz-Loyola is a symbol of the Ignatius family's Oñaz lineage, and is used by many <a href="/wiki/Society_of_Jesus" class="mw-redirect" title="Society of Jesus">Jesuit</a> institutions around the world. As the official colours of the Loyola family are <a href="/wiki/Maroon_(color)" class="mw-redirect" title="Maroon (color)">maroon</a> and <a href="/wiki/Gold_(color)" title="Gold (color)">gold</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> the Oñaz shield consists of seven maroon bars going diagonally from the upper left to the lower right on a goldfield. The bands were granted by the King of Spain to each of the Oñaz brothers, in recognition of their bravery in battle. The Loyola shield features a pair of <a href="/wiki/Rampant" class="mw-redirect" title="Rampant">rampant</a> <a href="/wiki/Gray_wolf" class="mw-redirect" title="Gray wolf">gray wolves</a> flanking each side of a cooking pot. The wolf was a symbol of nobility, while the entire design represented the family's generosity towards their military followers. According to legend, wolves had enough to feast on after the soldiers had eaten. Both shields were combined as a result of the intermarriage of the two families in 1261.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> Former coat of arms of the Argentine city, <a href="/wiki/Jun%C3%ADn,_Buenos_Aires" title="Junín, Buenos Aires">Junín, Buenos Aires</a> used until 1941 bore Loyola shield under the <a href="/wiki/Sun_of_May" title="Sun of May">Sun of May</a> and surrounded by laurel wreath.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Lineage">Lineage</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Loyola&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Lineage">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Villoslada established the following detailed genealogy of Ignatius of Loyola:<sup id="cite_ref-RGV_3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-RGV-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> 
</p>

<p><a href="/wiki/Mart%C3%ADn_Garc%C3%ADa_%C3%93%C3%B1ez_de_Loyola" title="Martín García Óñez de Loyola">Martín García Óñez de Loyola</a>, soldier and Governor of Chile killed by <a href="/wiki/Mapuche" title="Mapuche">Mapuches</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Curalaba" title="Battle of Curalaba">Battle of Curalaba</a>, is likely Ignatius's nephew.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<li><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFLoyola1992" class="citation book cs1">Loyola, (St.) Ignatius (1992). John Olin (ed.). <i>The Autobiography of St. Ignatius Loyola, with Related Documents</i>. New York: Fordham University Press. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/0-8232-1480-X" title="Special:BookSources/0-8232-1480-X"><bdi>0-8232-1480-X</bdi></a>.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=The+Autobiography+of+St.+Ignatius+Loyola%2C+with+Related+Documents&amp;rft.place=New+York&amp;rft.pub=Fordham+University+Press&amp;rft.date=1992&amp;rft.isbn=0-8232-1480-X&amp;rft.aulast=Loyola&amp;rft.aufirst=%28St.%29+Ignatius&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AIgnatius+of+Loyola" class="Z3988"></span></li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="See_also">See also</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Loyola&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: See also">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1132942124">.mw-parser-output .portalbox{padding:0;margin:0.5em 0;display:table;box-sizing:border-box;max-width:175px;list-style:none}.mw-parser-output .portalborder{border:solid #aaa 1px;padding:0.1em;background:#f9f9f9}.mw-parser-output .portalbox-entry{display:table-row;font-size:85%;line-height:110%;height:1.9em;font-style:italic;font-weight:bold}.mw-parser-output .portalbox-image{display:table-cell;padding:0.2em;vertical-align:middle;text-align:center}.mw-parser-output .portalbox-link{display:table-cell;padding:0.2em 0.2em 0.2em 0.3em;vertical-align:middle}@media(min-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .portalleft{clear:left;float:left;margin:0.5em 1em 0.5em 0}.mw-parser-output .portalright{clear:right;float:right;margin:0.5em 0 0.5em 1em}}</style><ul role="navigation" aria-label="Portals" class="noprint portalbox portalborder portalright">
<li class="portalbox-entry"><span class="portalbox-image"><span class="noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/48/Gloriole_blur.svg/32px-Gloriole_blur.svg.png" decoding="async" width="32" height="28" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/48/Gloriole_blur.svg/48px-Gloriole_blur.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/48/Gloriole_blur.svg/64px-Gloriole_blur.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="32" data-file-height="28" /></span></span></span><span class="portalbox-link"><a href="/wiki/Portal:Saints" title="Portal:Saints">Saints portal</a></span></li></ul>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Ignatian_Spirituality" class="mw-redirect" title="Ignatian Spirituality">Ignatian Spirituality</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/List_of_Jesuits" title="List of Jesuits">List of Jesuits</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Marie-Madeleine_d%27Hou%C3%ABt" title="Marie-Madeleine d&#39;Houët">Marie-Madeleine d'Houët</a> foundress of the Sisters, <a href="/wiki/Faithful_Companions_of_Jesus" title="Faithful Companions of Jesus">Faithful Companions of Jesus</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Mart%C3%ADn_Ignacio_de_Loyola" title="Martín Ignacio de Loyola">Martín Ignacio de Loyola</a></li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/Cave_of_Saint_Ignatius" title="Cave of Saint Ignatius">Cave of Saint Ignatius</a>, a sanctuary built where Ignatius of Loyola reflected for 11 months in a grotto, in <a href="/wiki/Manresa" title="Manresa">Manresa</a>.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Isabella_Roser" title="Isabella Roser">Isabella Roser</a> and <a href="/wiki/Isabel_de_Josa" title="Isabel de Josa">Isabel de Josa</a>, wealthy Catalan women who were Loyola's benefactors from the 1520s onwards.</li></ul>

<li id="cite_note-Verd1-12"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Verd1_12-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFVerd1976" class="citation journal cs1 cs1-prop-foreign-lang-source">Verd, Gabriel María (1976). "El "Íñigo" de San Ignacio de Loyola". <i>Archivum Historicum Societatis Iesu</i> (in Spanish). Roma: Institutum Historicum Societatis Iesu. <b>45</b>: 95–128. <a href="/wiki/ISSN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISSN (identifier)">ISSN</a>&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.worldcat.org/issn/0037-8887">0037-8887</a>.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=article&amp;rft.jtitle=Archivum+Historicum+Societatis+Iesu&amp;rft.atitle=El+%22%C3%8D%C3%B1igo%22+de+San+Ignacio+de+Loyola&amp;rft.volume=45&amp;rft.pages=95-128&amp;rft.date=1976&amp;rft.issn=0037-8887&amp;rft.aulast=Verd&amp;rft.aufirst=Gabriel+Mar%C3%ADa&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AIgnatius+of+Loyola" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Verd2-13"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-Verd2_13-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-Verd2_13-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFVerd1991" class="citation journal cs1 cs1-prop-foreign-lang-source">Verd, Gabriel María (1991). "De Iñigo a Ignacio. El cambio de nombre en San Ignacio de Loyola". <i>Archivum Historicum Societatis Iesu</i> (in Spanish). Roma: Institutum Historicum Societatis Iesu. <b>60</b>: 113–160. <a href="/wiki/ISSN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISSN (identifier)">ISSN</a>&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.worldcat.org/issn/0037-8887">0037-8887</a>. <q>That St. Ignatius of Loyola's name was changed is a known fact, but it cannot be said that it is widely known in the historiography of the saint — neither the characteristics of the names Iñigo and Ignacio nor the reasons for the change. It is first necessary to make clear the meaning of the names; they are distinct, despite the persistently held opinion in onomastic (dictionaries) and popular thought. In Spain Ignacio and Iñigo are at times used interchangeably just as if they were Jacobo and Jaime. With reference to the name Iñigo, it is fitting to give some essential notions to eliminate ambiguities and help understand what follows. This name first appears on the Ascoli brome (dated November 18, 90 BC), in a list of Spanish knights belonging to a Turma salluitana or Saragossan. It speaks of Elandus Enneces f[ilius], and according to Menéndez Pidal, the final «s» is the «z» of Spanish patronymics and could be nothing other than Elando Iñiguez. It is an ancestral Hispanic name. Ignacio, on the other hand, is a Latin name. In classical Latin, there is Egnatius with an initial E. It appears only twice with an initial I (Ignatius) in the sixty volumes of the Corpus Inscriptionum Latinarum. This late Latin and Greek form prevailed. In the classical period, Egnatius was used as a nomen (gentilitial name) and not as a praenomen (first name) or cognomen (surname), except in very rare cases. (...) The most important conclusion, perhaps unexpected, but not unknown, is that St. Ignatius did not change his name. That is to say, he did not intend to change it. What he did was to adopt for France and Italy a name which he believed was a simple variant of his own, and which was more acceptable among foreigners.... If he had remained in Spain, he would have, without doubt, remained Iñigo.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=article&amp;rft.jtitle=Archivum+Historicum+Societatis+Iesu&amp;rft.atitle=De+I%C3%B1igo+a+Ignacio.+El+cambio+de+nombre+en+San+Ignacio+de+Loyola&amp;rft.volume=60&amp;rft.pages=113-160&amp;rft.date=1991&amp;rft.issn=0037-8887&amp;rft.aulast=Verd&amp;rft.aufirst=Gabriel+Mar%C3%ADa&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AIgnatius+of+Loyola" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>

Retrieved <span class="nowrap">20 June</span> 2021</span>.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=unknown&amp;rft.jtitle=Encyclopedia+Britannica&amp;rft.atitle=Saint+Ignatius+of+Loyola%3A+Biography%2C+Patron+Saint+Of%2C+Feast+Day%2C+%26+Facts&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.britannica.com%2Fbiography%2FSt-Ignatius-of-Loyola&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AIgnatius+of+Loyola" class="Z3988"></span></span>

<li id="cite_note-34"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-34">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFLonghurst1957" class="citation journal cs1">Longhurst, John E.
